BY   ANDREWS   &    Co.,    DURHAM  ; 

WHITTAKER  &  Co.,  2,  WHITE   HART  STREET,   PATERNOSTER 

SQUARE ; 

BERNARD    QUARITCH,    15,    PICCADILLY  ; 

BLACKWOOD   &   SONS,    EDINBURGH. 

1898. 


At  a  Meeting  of  the  Council  of  the  SURTEES  SOCIETY, 
held  in  Durham  Castle  on  June  ist,  1897,  the  DEAN  OF 
DURHAM  in  the  chair. 

It  was  resolved, 

That  a  Volume  of  Extracts  from  the  Rolls  of  the 
Officials  of  the  Priory  of  Durham  should  be  edited  by 
CANON  FOWLER. 

And  at  a  similar  Meeting,  held  on  December  yth,  1897. 

It  was  ordered, 

That  a  Volume  of  Extracts  from  the  Rolls  of  the 
Officials  of  the  Priory  of  Durham,  to  be  edited  by  CANON 
FOWLER,  be  one  of  the  Volumes  for  the  year  1898. 

WILLIAM    BROWN, 

Secretary. 


PREFACE. 


THE  history  of  the  publication  of  these  Extracts  is  as 
follows.  Some  three  or  four  years  ago  the  Editor  had 
almost  completed,  for  the  Surtees  Society,  an  annotated 
edition  of  the  Rites  of  Durham,  when  his  attention  was 
directed  by  Canon  Greenwell  to  some  post-Reformation 
documents  that  seemed  likely  to  become  serviceable  in 
connexion  with  Rites.  This  suggestion  proved  to  be 
well-founded,  but  it  led  to  an  examination  of  the  later  of 
the  pre-Reformation  Rolls,  and  this  further  research  was 
yet  more  productive.  Next  it  was  found  that  all  the  Rolls 
must  be  gone  through,  for  there  was  no  saying  what 
valuable  illustrations  they  might  not  afford.  The  first  idea 
of  extracts  included  in  the  notes  on  Rites  soon  grew  into 
that  of  an  Appendix.  Then  it  proved  that  such  Appendix 
must  be,  like  that  to  the  Scriptores  Tres,  larger  than 
the  book  itself,  and  so  it  was  found  that  the  Account- 
Rolls  must  be  issued  as  an  independent  volume,  which 
could  be  referred  to  in  the  notes  on  Rites.  This 
volume  has  finally  grown  into  three,  which  will  be  issued 
with  one  continuous  pagination,  and  with  a  general  index 
and  glossary  to  the  whole  at  the  end  of  Vol.  III.  With 
that  volume  will  also  be  issued  something  by  way  of 
general  introduction,  if  space  permit. 

The  Surtees  Society  has  already  issued  the  Durham 
Household  Book,  1530-34,  and  Inventories  and  Account- 
Rolls  of  the  Cells  of  Finchale,  Coldingham,  Jarrowr,  and 
Monkwearmouth.  Dr.  Raine  has  given  translations  of 
extracts  from  the  Feretrars'  and  other  Rolls  in  his  St. 
Cuthbert,  and  in  his  Brief  Account  of  Durham  Cathedral. 
Mr.  Blakiston  has  recently  edited  extracts  from  the  Account- 
Rolls  of  Durham  College  for  the  Oxford  Historical  Society. 
But  the  rich  mine  of  the  extant  Rolls  of  the  great  Abbey  of 
Durham  itself  has  hitherto  remained  for  the  most  part 
unworked, 


viii  PREFACE. 

The  Editor  has  done  the  best  that  he  could  with  so 
vast  a  mass  of  material  in  the  space  of  time  that  could  be 
devoted  to  it.  It  has  been,  of  course,  out  of  the  question 
to  give  more  than  a  very  few  rolls  in  extenso,  by  way  of 
specimens.  In  the  case  of  the  others,  those  particulars 
which  seemed  of  most  interest  or  importance  have  been 
extracted  under  their  various  heads.  In  such  a  work  it  has 
been  difficult  to  avoid  some  needless  repetitions,  and  it  is 
to  be  feared  that  some  things  may  have  been  omitted  that 
ought  to  have  been  included.  But  the  original  rolls  will 
remain  at  the  service  of  any  scholars  who  may  be  stimulated 
to  further  research,  and,  should  the  gleaning  of  the  grapes 
ever  prove  to  be  better  than  the  vintage,  no  one  would 
be  better  pleased  than  the  present  Editor.  It  is  believed, 
however,  that  the  flavour  of  the  latter  will  be  found  quite 
equal  to  that  of  anything  of  the  same  kind  that  has 
hitherto  been  provided  for  such  as  care  for  these  matters. 

No  Roll  has  been  left  without  mention  of  some  kind, 
and  the  following  pages  include,  at  any  rate,  a  complete 
calendar  of  the  whole  series,  from  which  lists  of  the  various 
obedientaries  or  monastic  officers  might  easily  be  compiled. 

The  Table  of  Contents  will  shew  what  is  to  be 
found  in  the  present  volume.  Vol.  II  will  include  the 
Commoners',  Terrars',  Stock-keepers',  Marescalcia  Prioris, 
Sacrists',  and  Feretrars'  Rolls,  most  of  which  matter  is 
already  in  type.  Vol.  Ill  will  contain  the  Bursars'  Rolls 
and  other  matter,  now  ready  to  be  printed  whenever  the 
Society  can  undertake  it.  And  the  Editor  hopes  that,  when 
that  is  out,  he  \vill  be  able  at  once  to  complete  the 
preparation  of  the  long-expected  volume  on  the  Rites  of 
Durham. 


WiNTERTON,    DONCASTER, 

JULY,  1898.

The  above  is  the  first  week  in  which  no  flesh-meat  or 
fowl  appears.  In  the  previous  week  all  is  fish  except  i 
porco,  i  vitulo,  10  porcellis,  et  i  gallina.  We  find  nothing 
but  fish  in  the  three  remaining  weeks  ;  in  week  36,  "  In 
i  libra  de  sucore,  9^." 

The  total  length  of  this  roll  is  about  8  ft.  9  in.,  but  the 
third  and  fourth  skins  have  never  been  sewn  on.  On  the 
back  are  lists  of  sums  due  to  a  number  of  persons,  and  of 
wages.

1  Mr.  Percy  J.  Heavvood  has  ascertained  that  the  eclipse  of  January 
3 ist,  A.p.  1310,  was  an  annular  one.  According-  to  his  calculation,  the  time 
of  maximum  obscuration  at  Durham  was  i  h.  7  m.  p.m.,  and  the  moon 
would  then  have  covered  nearly  seven-eighths  of  the  sun's  disc,  leaving  a 
small  crescent  visible  on  the  upper  and  western  side  of  the  dark  face  of  the 
moon.  The  eclipse  would  thus  present  a  striking,  though  not  annular, 
appearance  at  Durham,  and  a  considerable  amount  of  darkness  would  be 
caused.

1414.     Tho.  Moreby.

interest.  The  following  entries  occur.

c.  1420.  A  long  paper  roll  for  six  months  and  three  weeks, 
consisting  of  nine  pieces  of  paper  neatly  sewn  together 
like  a  parchment  roll.  Water-mark,  a  trefoil  leaf  with 
two  bracts  on  the  stalk.  The  roll  is  imperfect  at  the 
top,  beginning  near  the  end  of  the  second  week.  The 
marginal  notes  are  unusually  full,  but  there  are  very 
few  special  entries  relating  to  provisions.

Status,  Hugo  de  Shirburn,  1383. 

Relates  wholly  to  plate  and  bedding,  etc.,  except  two 
lines  at  the  end  about  stable.  Begins  with  the  silver  piece 
and  cover  ;  then  follow  about  fifty  items,  including  many 
of  the  articles  mentioned  in  earlier  lists,  but  seemingly 
nothing  else  of  special  interest  except  as  showing  increase 
in  number  of  articles.  It  is  in  small  writing,  injured  by 
damp,  not  very  easy  to  read,  and  would  take  a  long  time  to 
copy.  On  the  back  are  many  single  letters  written  again 
and  again,  as  if  to  try  pens  or  practise  writing,  and  a  few 
words,  as  "  omnibus  omnia  non  mea  somnia."

ROTULI    MAGISTRORUM 
INFIRMARIE. 


These  rolls  are  very  short,  dealing  with  about  £10 
per  annum.  But  they  contain  many  interesting  entries. 

The  first  is  much  decayed  and  faded;  indented  on  top; 
the  date  "  Mill'mo  ccclijdo  "  can  just  be  discerned  in  the 
heading,  and  on  the  back  is  written  in  an  old  but  later 
hand,  "  M.  de  Chilton  1352." 

It  begins  with  a  Status  or  list  of  goods,  in  which  can 
be  made  out  :  —  ij  paria  linthiaminum  pro  altare,  ij  missalia 
defectiva,  ij  manutergia,  j  calefactorium,  j  pixid'  ebur',  .  .

sawnapes,  iiij  rotund',  xx  coclear'  argent',  ij  ciphi  murrei, 
xij  quissynes  unius  secte  et  xj  alius  secte,  v  pelves,  iiij

pro  pomis,  x  cathedre  lignee,  j  grydel  pro  pane,  iij  .  .  .

dono  ejusdem,  tria  gradalia,  duo  mortuaria,  unum  anti- 
phonarium  non  notatum,  duo  psalteria,  unum  collectare,

stole,  unum  manucale,  due  phiole  stannee  pro  vino  et  aqua. 
In  Aula,  duo  bancalia  vetera,  due  mense,  una  parva  mensa 
pro  infirmis  in  camera,  tria  paria  trestellorum,  duo  sawnaps,
